(44)

We note that the string diagram in
\overline{\mathchoice{Q^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.28568pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.47905pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\kappa}}}_{{{\kappa^{\prime}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{Q^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.28568pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.47905pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\kappa}}}_{{{\kappa^{\prime}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{Q^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.28568pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.47905pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\kappa}}}_{{{\kappa^{\prime}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{Q^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.28568pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.47905pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\kappa}}}_{{{\kappa^{\prime}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}}
is the same as the one for
\mathchoice{Q^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\kappa^{\prime}}}}_{{{\kappa}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.28568pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.47905pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{Q^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\kappa^{\prime}}}}_{{{\kappa}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.28568pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.47905pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{Q^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\kappa^{\prime}}}}_{{{\kappa}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.28568pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.47905pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{Q^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\kappa^{\prime}}}}_{{{\kappa}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.28568pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.47905pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}
in Eq. (
24
) due to the mirror symmetry for unitary
\mathcal{C}
, i.e. taking the complex conjugate of a string diagram corresponds to flipping the diagram along the horizontal axis (while not flipping the arrows). Therefore, complex conjugation of the string diagram reverses the swap of the labels
\kappa,\kappa^{\prime}
in
\overline{\mathchoice{Q^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.28568pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.47905pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\kappa}}}_{{{\kappa^{\prime}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{Q^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.28568pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.47905pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\kappa}}}_{{{\kappa^{\prime}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{Q^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.28568pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.47905pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\kappa}}}_{{{\kappa^{\prime}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{Q^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.28568pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.47905pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\kappa}}}_{{{\kappa^{\prime}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}}
. Furthermore, we notice that by construction, all
\alpha_{\ell}\in\mathbb{R}
, see Eq. (
32
). Thus, we conclude that
\mathchoice{Q^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\kappa^{\prime}}}}_{{{\kappa}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.28568pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.47905pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{Q^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\kappa^{\prime}}}}_{{{\kappa}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.28568pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.47905pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{Q^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\kappa^{\prime}}}}_{{{\kappa}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.28568pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.47905pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{Q^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\kappa^{\prime}}}}_{{{\kappa}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.28568pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.47905pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}=\overline{\mathchoice{Q^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.28568pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.47905pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\kappa}}}_{{{\kappa^{\prime}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{Q^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.28568pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.47905pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\kappa}}}_{{{\kappa^{\prime}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{Q^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.28568pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.47905pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\kappa}}}_{{{\kappa^{\prime}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{Q^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.28568pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.47905pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\kappa}}}_{{{\kappa^{\prime}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}}
, which implies that
\hat{Q}
is a Hermitian matrix. The same argumentation can be followed for the volume operator
\tilde{Q}
that involves the
j_{2}
–
j_{3}
coupling.

IV.  Recovering the Standard
\mathrm{SU}(2)
Volume Operator

In this section, we show that our construction recovers the standard volume operator for
\mathrm{SU}(2)
, see Eq. (
12
) and Eq. (
14
). For this, we consider the input category
\mathrm{SU}(2)_{k}
, which is the
q
-deformed version of
\mathrm{SU}(2)
with deformation parameter
q=\mathrm{e}^{\frac{2\pi\mathrm{i}}{k+2}}
. In the limit
k\rightarrow\infty
, we have
q\rightarrow 1
and recover
\mathrm{SU}(2)
. The categorical data of
\mathrm{SU}(2)_{k}
can be found in Ref.
[
45
]
or Ref.
[
56
]
.
\mathrm{SU}(2)_{k}
has simple objects
I=\big\{\frac{1}{2},1,\frac{3}{2},\dots k\big\}
. With

[n]_{q}=\frac{q^{\frac{n}{2}}-q^{-\frac{n}{2}}}{q^{\frac{1}{2}}-q^{-\frac{1}{2}}},\quad n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},

(45)

being a
q
-deformed integer, we have
\alpha_{\ell}=[\ell(\ell+1)]_{q}
. In the limit
k\rightarrow\infty
,
\alpha_{\ell}\rightarrow\ell(\ell+1)
. The
R
-matrices read

\displaystyle R_{j}^{j_{1}j_{2}}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{j-j_{1}-j_{2}}q^{\frac{1}{2}\big(j(j+1)-j_{1}(j_{1}+1)-j_{2}(j_{2}+1)\big)}

\displaystyle\rightarrow(-1)^{j-j_{1}-j_{2}}

(46)

and the quantum dimensions become

d_{j}=\frac{\sin\left(\frac{(2j+1)\pi}{k+2}\right)}{\sin\left(\frac{\pi}{k+2}\right)}\rightarrow 2j+1.

(47)

In the same way, we evaluate the limit
k\rightarrow\infty
for the
F
-symbols, which yields

\displaystyle\big(F_{j}^{j_{1}j_{2}j_{3}}\big)_{j_{12}j_{23}}\rightarrow

\displaystyle(-1)^{j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}+j}\sqrt{(2j_{12}+1)(2j_{23}+1)}

\displaystyle\begin{Bmatrix}j_{1}&j_{2}&j_{12}\\
j_{3}&j&j_{23}\end{Bmatrix}.

(48)

Note that the
F
-symbols for
\mathrm{SU}(2)_{k}
are always real for all
k
and, in addition, all simple objects are self-dual, i.e.
j=j^{*}
for all
j\in I
. The same properties also hold for
\mathrm{SU}(2)
. Since
\mathrm{SU}(2)_{k}
is unitary, we can take the limit
k\rightarrow\infty
of Eq. (
30
). This gives

\displaystyle\mathchoice{Q^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\kappa^{\prime}}}}_{{{\kappa}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.28568pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.47905pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{Q^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\kappa^{\prime}}}}_{{{\kappa}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.28568pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.47905pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{Q^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\kappa^{\prime}}}}_{{{\kappa}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.28568pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.47905pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{Q^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\kappa^{\prime}}}}_{{{\kappa}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.28568pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.47905pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\rightarrow

\displaystyle-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{4}[\kappa(\kappa+1)-\kappa^{\prime}(\kappa^{\prime}+1)](-1)^{\kappa+\kappa^{\prime}}(-1)^{2(j_{4}-j_{2})}

\displaystyle(2j_{4}+1)(2\kappa+1)(2\kappa^{\prime}+1)\begin{Bmatrix}\kappa^{\prime}&j_{3}&j_{4}\\
j_{4}&0&\kappa^{\prime}\end{Bmatrix}

\displaystyle\begin{Bmatrix}\kappa&j_{3}&j_{4}\\
j_{4}&0&\kappa\end{Bmatrix}\sum_{m,n\in\mathcal{L}_{23}}(-1)^{m+n}(2m+1)(2n+1)

\displaystyle\quad\begin{Bmatrix}j_{1}&j_{2}&\kappa^{\prime}\\
j_{3}&j_{4}&m\end{Bmatrix}\begin{Bmatrix}j_{1}&j_{2}&\kappa\\
j_{3}&j_{4}&n\end{Bmatrix}\sum_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{13}}\ell(\ell+1)(2\ell+1)

\displaystyle\qquad\begin{Bmatrix}j_{3}&j_{1}&\ell\\
j_{4}&j_{2}&m\end{Bmatrix}\begin{Bmatrix}j_{3}&j_{1}&\ell\\
j_{4}&j_{2}&n\end{Bmatrix},

(49)

where
\mathcal{L}_{13}=\{|j_{1}-j_{3}|,\dots,j_{1}+j_{3}\}\cap\{|j_{2}-j_{4}|,\dots,j_{2}+j_{4}\}
. Now, we use the following identity for
6j
-symbols involving a zero charge
[
57
, Ch. 9]
:

\begin{Bmatrix}b&a&c\\
c&0&b\end{Bmatrix}=\begin{Bmatrix}a&b&c\\
0&c&b\end{Bmatrix}=\frac{(-1)^{a+b+c}}{\sqrt{(2b+1)(2c+1)}},

(50)

where the first step follows from the fact that the
6j
-symbols are invariant under permutation of their columns. This leads to

\displaystyle\mathchoice{Q^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\kappa^{\prime}}}}_{{{\kappa}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.28568pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.47905pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{Q^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\kappa^{\prime}}}}_{{{\kappa}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.28568pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.47905pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{Q^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\kappa^{\prime}}}}_{{{\kappa}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.28568pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.47905pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{Q^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\kappa^{\prime}}}}_{{{\kappa}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.28568pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.47905pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\rightarrow

\displaystyle-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{4}[\kappa(\kappa+1)-\kappa^{\prime}(\kappa^{\prime}+1)](-1)^{\kappa+\kappa^{\prime}}(-1)^{2(j_{3}-j_{2})}

\displaystyle\sqrt{(2\kappa+1)(2\kappa^{\prime}+1)}\sum_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{13}}\ell(\ell+1)(2\ell+1)(-1)^{-2\ell}

\displaystyle\sum_{m\in\mathcal{L}_{23}}(-1)^{m+\ell+\kappa^{\prime}}(2m+1)\begin{Bmatrix}j_{3}&j_{2}&m\\
j_{1}&j_{4}&\kappa^{\prime}\end{Bmatrix}\begin{Bmatrix}j_{1}&j_{4}&m\\
j_{2}&j_{3}&\ell\end{Bmatrix}

\displaystyle\sum_{n\in\mathcal{L}_{23}}(-1)^{n+\ell+\kappa}(2n+1)\begin{Bmatrix}j_{3}&j_{2}&n\\
j_{1}&j_{4}&\kappa\end{Bmatrix}\begin{Bmatrix}j_{1}&j_{4}&n\\
j_{2}&j_{3}&\ell\end{Bmatrix},

where we again used invariance under permutation of columns and also that the
6j
-symbols are invariant under simultaneous exchange of upper and lower labels in two columns. By inspection of the set
\mathcal{L}
, we notice that the factor
(-1)^{2\ell}
can be rewritten as
(-1)^{2\ell}=(-1)^{2(j_{2}+j_{4})}
. The next step is to use another identity of the
6j
-symbols, namely
[
57
, Ch. 9]

\sum_{x}(-1)^{f+g+x}(2x+1)\begin{Bmatrix}a&b&x\\
c&d&f\end{Bmatrix}\begin{Bmatrix}c&d&x\\
b&a&g\end{Bmatrix}=\begin{Bmatrix}a&d&f\\
b&c&g\end{Bmatrix}.

Thus, we finally arrive at

\displaystyle\mathchoice{Q^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\kappa^{\prime}}}}_{{{\kappa}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.28568pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.47905pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{Q^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\kappa^{\prime}}}}_{{{\kappa}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.28568pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.47905pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{Q^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\kappa^{\prime}}}}_{{{\kappa}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.28568pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.47905pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{Q^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\kappa^{\prime}}}}_{{{\kappa}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.28568pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.47905pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\rightarrow

\displaystyle-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{4}(-1)^{\kappa+\kappa^{\prime}}(-1)^{2(j_{3}+j_{4})}[\kappa(\kappa+1)-\kappa^{\prime}(\kappa^{\prime}+1)]

\displaystyle\sqrt{(2\kappa+1)(2\kappa^{\prime}+1)}\sum_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{13}}\ell(\ell+1)(2\ell+1)

\displaystyle\begin{Bmatrix}j_{3}&j_{4}&\kappa^{\prime}\\
j_{2}&j_{1}&\ell\end{Bmatrix}\begin{Bmatrix}j_{3}&j_{4}&\kappa\\
j_{2}&j_{1}&\ell\end{Bmatrix}.

(51)

A similar calculation shows that the
j_{2}
–
j_{3}
volume operator
\tilde{Q}
has a limit

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\tilde{Q}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\kappa^{\prime}}}}_{{{\kappa}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.28568pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.47905pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\tilde{Q}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\kappa^{\prime}}}}_{{{\kappa}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.28568pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.47905pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\tilde{Q}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\kappa^{\prime}}}}_{{{\kappa}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.28568pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.47905pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\tilde{Q}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\kappa^{\prime}}}}_{{{\kappa}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.52924pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.28568pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.47905pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\rightarrow

\displaystyle-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{4}(-1)^{2(\kappa+\kappa^{\prime})}(-1)^{2(j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}+j_{4})}

\displaystyle[\kappa(\kappa+1)-\kappa^{\prime}(\kappa^{\prime}+1)]\sqrt{(2\kappa+1)(2\kappa^{\prime}+1)}

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{23}}\ell(\ell+1)(2\ell+1)\begin{Bmatrix}j_{1}&j_{2}&\kappa^{\prime}\\
j_{3}&j_{4}&\ell\end{Bmatrix}\begin{Bmatrix}j_{1}&j_{2}&\kappa\\
j_{3}&j_{4}&\ell\end{Bmatrix}.

(52)

Comparison with Eq. (
14
) shows that the
j_{2}
–
j_{3}
volume operator
\tilde{Q}
(
52
) coincides with the standard
\mathrm{SU}(2)
volume operator (
14
) up to a phase factor of
(-1)^{2(\kappa+\kappa^{\prime}+j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}+j_{4})}
in the limit
k\rightarrow\infty
. Likewise, the
j_{1}
–
j_{3}
volume operator
\hat{Q}
(
51
) coincides with the standard
\mathrm{SU}(2)
volume operator (
14
) up to a phase factor of
(-1)^{\kappa+\kappa^{\prime}+2(j_{3}+j_{4})}
in the limit
k\rightarrow\infty
.

V.  Spectrum of the
\mathrm{SU}(2)_{k}
Volume Operator

The volume operator is a physical observable quantifying the volume of the three-dimensional spatial component of discrete spacetime. Therefore, the main interest is its spectral properties. The spectral analysis of the
\mathrm{SU}(2)
volume operator has been carried out in Refs.
[
7
,
58
,
59
,
11
,
11
]
. By restricting the possible
\mathrm{SU}(2)
irreducible representation labels of each tetrahedron to a value up to
k
, we obtain a categorical volume operator with input category
\mathrm{SU}(2)_{k}
. As shown in Sec.
IV
, this volume operator coincides with the
\mathrm{SU}(2)
volume operator in the limit
k\rightarrow\infty
. In this section, we study the changes in the (tetrahedron) volume’s spectrum due to the deformation
k
, while keeping the
j
labels attached to the tetrahedron faces fixed. We also study how the spectrum varies with
k
for different values of the fixed
j
’s. To this end, we fix two irreducible representation labels
j_{1}=j_{2}
and vary
j_{3}=j_{4}=j_{\mathrm{max}}
. This is analogous to the analysis carried out in Ref.
[
11
]
. We remark that in the context of loop quantum gravity, fixing the values of the
j
labels corresponds to fixing the areas of the tetrahedron’s faces (as measured by the
q
-deformed area operator
[
29
]
).

For
j_{1}=j_{2}=\frac{1}{2}
, the volume operator becomes a
2\times 2
matrix, which we diagonalize. We find that the spectrum of the standard
\mathrm{SU}(2)
volume operator and the generalized
\mathrm{SU}(2)_{k}
volume operator are close to each other already for a moderately low value of
k
, see Fig.
2
. However, if
j_{\mathrm{max}}
increases, the two spectra are significantly different for small
k
. In particular, the
\mathrm{SU}(2)_{k}
volume operator admits smaller eigenvalues than the standard
\mathrm{SU}(2)
volume operator. We find the same behaviour for the case where
j_{1}=j_{2}=1
, see Fig.
3
. This indicates that in spacetime regions with considerably different irreducible representation labels
j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4}
but relatively small maximum
j_{\mathrm{max}}
, the standard
\mathrm{SU}(2)
volume operator tends to overestimate the volume. This might be due to weights in the volume from non-contributing higher irreducible representations.

Figure 2:
Eigenvalues of the quantum volume operator
\hat{Q}
for
\mathrm{SU}(2)_{k}
for different
k
. We fix the input labels to
j_{1}=j_{2}=\frac{1}{2}
and show the eigenvalues of
\hat{Q}
for different values of
j_{3}=j_{4}=j_{\text{max}}
represented by the coloured dots. In addition, we plot the corresponding eigenvalues of the
\mathrm{SU}(2)
volume operator (indicated by the constant horizontal lines), which coincide with the
\mathrm{SU}(2)_{k}
eigenvalues in the limit
k\rightarrow\infty
.

Figure 3:
Eigenvalues of the quantum volume operator
\hat{Q}
for
\mathrm{SU}(2)_{k}
for different
k
. We fix the input labels to
j_{1}=j_{2}=1
and show the nonzero eigenvalues of
\hat{Q}
for different values of
j_{3}=j_{4}=j_{\text{max}}
represented by the coloured dots. Except for the case where
j_{\text{max}}=\frac{1}{2}
,
\hat{Q}
is a
3\times 3
matrix and always admits an eigenvalue of zero. This behaviour is the same as for the
\mathrm{SU}(2)
volume operator. The case
j_{\text{max}}=\frac{1}{2}
is the same as as
j_{\text{max}}=1
in Fig.
2
, cf. the orange dots and lines therein. In addition, we plot the corresponding eigenvalues of the
\mathrm{SU}(2)
volume operator (indicated by the constant horizontal lines), which coincide with the
\mathrm{SU}(2)_{k}
eigenvalues in the limit
k\rightarrow\infty
.

VI.  Discussion and Conclusions

In condensed matter physics, the categorification of spin models such as the Heisenberg model and certain lattice gauge theories has given rise to several physically rich anyonic lattice models that realize both conventional and exotic quantum phases of matter. Motivated by these developments, here we have introduced a categorification of the quantum volume operator [Sec.
III
] that plays a fundamental role in the description of discrete spacetime geometries underlying the formulation of background-independent candidate theories of quantum gravity such as LQG.

As discussed in the introduction, quantizing general relativity with a cosmological constant results in a
q
-deformation of LQG. However, a
q
-deformed volume operator has not been previously derived. In this work, we have derived a
q
-deformed volume operator using minimal physical assumptions, primarily driven by identifying and suitably generalizing the category-theoretical structures underlying the original construction of the SU(2) volume operator.

Subsequently, we have emphasized the physical relevance of various mathematical properties of category-theoretic objects and identified fusion categories — a natural generalization of the representations of finite groups — as the main ingredient in the construction. We have first constructed a categorified volume operator for ribbon fusion categories, which exhibit tetrahedral symmetry, [Subsec.
III.2
] and then for more general spherical fusion categories without tetrahedral symmetry [Subsec.
III.3
]. We have shown that as long as the category is unitary, the corresponding generalized volume operator is guaranteed to be Hermitian [Subsec.
III.4
]. As an example, we consider the
q
-deformed group
\mathrm{SU}(2)_{k}
. After demonstrating that the standard
\mathrm{SU}(2)
volume operator is recovered in the limit
k\to\infty
[Sec.
IV
], we then compared the spectrum of the
q
-deformed
\mathrm{SU}(2)_{k}
volume operator to that of its
\mathrm{SU}(2)
counterpart [Sec.
V
].

An interesting direction for future research is to investigate the relationship between the
k
parameter in our categorified volume operator and the cosmological constant in loop quantum gravity (LQG). Since our construction does not proceed from the quantization of classical gravity with the cosmological constant, it is unclear whether the
k
(and thus
q
) parameter in our volume operator directly corresponds to the cosmological constant.

To establish a connection between
k
and the cosmological constant, one could adopt an approach similar to that used in Ponzano-Regge geometries using a fixed triangulation of the spacetime manifold labeled by
SU(2)_{k}
spins as discussed in Sec.
I
.
In the large spin limit, this resembles a modified Regge action that includes an explicit volume term, akin to the Einstein-Hilbert action with a cosmological constant (see e.g., Refs.
[
29
,
30
]
). These calculations are performed in the small deformation limit,
k\gg 1
, which also regularizes the partition function. The partition function is proportional to a product over
6j
-symbols for all triangulation labellings and would diverge without a cutoff on the irreducible representations.

Our approach differs significantly as it is non-perturbative and uses a spin-network geometry instead of the Regge geometry, where the connection to an Einstein-Hilbert-like action is less clear. For instance, unlike in the Regge case, where the partition function sums over all admissible spin labelings of tetrahedra edges, a spin network also includes quantum degrees of freedom at the nodes (the intertwiners), which must also be considered.

Appendix: Basic Concepts in Category Theory

In this appendix, we introduce the basic category theoretical concepts that appear in the main text of this article. We will often not provide the full technical definitions, but instead focus on the implications of a categorical property to the graphical calculus of string diagrams. For an elementary physics-friendly introduction to category theory, we refer the reader to Refs.
[
41
,
42
]
. A thorough and exhaustive mathematical introduction is provided in Ref.
[
43
]
.

In the following, we denote a category with
\mathcal{C}
and assume that it is small.
4
4

4

A category is called
small
if both the class of objects and the class of morphisms are sets.
A category consists of objects and morphisms, which are maps between the objects. We denote the set of morphisms between objects
a,b\in\mathcal{C}
as
\mathrm{hom}(a,b)
and refer to it as hom-set. Graphically, we denote objects
a\in\mathcal{C}
by oriented labeled strings
a

and morphisms
f:a\rightarrow b
between objects
a,b\in\mathcal{C}
by circles with an input string
a
and an output string
b
f
a
b

.

The composition
g\circ f:a\rightarrow c
of two morphisms
g:b\rightarrow c
and
f:a\rightarrow b
is given by stacking the circles and connecting the compatible strings
f
a
g
b
c

.

The composition of morphisms is associative by definition.

We are interested in categories that carry a generalized tensor product structure. In category theoretical terms, this is called a
monoidal structure
and comprises the following data:

(i)

a tensor product functor
\otimes:\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
;

(ii)

a unit object
0\in\mathcal{C}
;

(iii)

two natural isomorphisms called the left- and right-unitor such that
0\otimes a\simeq a\simeq a\otimes 0
for all objects
a\in\mathcal{C}
;

(iv)

a natural isomorphism called the associator such that
(a\otimes b)\otimes c\simeq a\otimes(b\otimes c)
for each triple of objects
a,b,c\in\mathcal{C}
.

These structures satisfy certain consistency relations, see for instance Ref.
[
41
, Def. 3.2]
. Most notably, the associators satisfy the pentagon identity. If
\mathcal{C}
carries a monoidal structure, it is referred to as a
monoidal category
. We remark that without loss of generality, we always work with
skeletal
monoidal categories, see Ref.
[
43
, Ch. 2.8]
. This is standard in the physics literature. In terms of string diagrams, the tensor product
a\otimes b
of two objects
a,b\in\mathcal{C}
is denoted by writing the objects next to each other
a
b

.

The next concept we introduce is that of a
braiding
. For a monoidal category
\mathcal{C}
, this is a family of natural isomorphisms
B_{a,b}:a\otimes b\rightarrow b\otimes a
. The
B_{a,b}
satisfy certain consistency relations, which can be found for instance in Ref.
[
41
, Def. 3.5]
. Furthermore, they satisfy the
Yang–Baxter equation
. Graphically, the braiding
B_{a,b}
is denoted by
b
a

and its inverse
B^{-1}_{a,b}
is
b
a

.

If the braiding is its own inverse, i.e. if
B_{a,b}=B_{b,a}^{-1}
, then we call the category
symmetric
.

We will now assume that for all objects
a,b\in\mathcal{C}
the hom-set
\mathrm{hom}(a,b)
of morphisms from
a
to
b
is a vector space over the field
\Bbbk
. Furthermore, let the composition of morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
be bilinear with respect to
\Bbbk
. A category with these properties is called
\Bbbk
-linear
. An object
a\in\mathcal{C}
in a
\Bbbk
-linear category
\mathcal{C}
is called
simple
if
\mathrm{hom}(a,a)=\Bbbk
. We can then define the notion of a
fusion category
, in which simple objects play a special role. That is, we assume
\mathcal{C}
to be a monoidal
\Bbbk
-linear category and further assume the existence of a set
I
of simple objects in
\mathcal{C}
. The set
I
is assumed to satisfy the following three conditions:

(i)

the unit object is an element of
I
, i.e.
0\in I
;

(ii)

\mathrm{hom}(a,b)=0
for all simple objects
a,b\in I
with
a\neq b
;

(iii)

any object
a\in\mathcal{C}
can be written as a finite direct sum of simple objects, i.e.
a=\bigoplus_{i\in I}i
.

Combining conditions (ii) and (iii) implies that all hom-sets
\mathrm{hom}(a,b)
are finite-dimensional vector spaces and that for all
a,b\in\mathcal{C}
,
\mathrm{hom}(a,b)=\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathrm{hom}(a,i)\otimes\mathrm{hom}(i,b)

[
49
, Ch. 4.4.1]
. Due to this, we sometimes call the hom-sets
fusion spaces
. In fusion categories, we can define the action of a fusion morphism
t:a\otimes b\rightarrow c
, which acts as

a\otimes b=\sum_{c\in I}N_{ab}^{c}\,c.

Here, the
N_{ab}^{c}
are nonnegative integers known as
fusion multiplicities
. They specify the number of different ways in which two objects
a,b
may be fused to a third object
c
. We denote the hom-set of a fusion product
V_{ab}^{c}
, such that
\mathrm{dim}(V_{ab}^{c})=N_{ab}^{c}
. Analogously, we can define a splitting morphism
t^{\prime}:c\rightarrow a\otimes b
with corresponding splitting hom-set
V_{c}^{ab}
. In terms of string diagrams, fusion and splitting are represented by trivalent vertices
t
a
b
c
and
t^{\prime}
a
b
c

.

Since the hom-sets in fusion categories are vector spaces, we can fix a basis. For
V^{a_{1},...,a_{m}}_{a^{\prime}_{1},...,a^{\prime}_{n}}
, the standard basis is given by fusion diagrams of the form

(53)

To simplify the notation, we do not indicate the fusion and splitting morphisms
t,t^{\prime}
explicitly here and will keep omitting them in what follows. In addition, we will also omit fusion multiplicities. However, all diagrams can be made general by adding a label at each trivalent vertex indicating the way the fusion was performed. By fixing the basis, the associators become matrices, called
F-symbols

F_{d}^{abc}:\mathrm{hom}(d,(a\otimes b)\otimes c)\rightarrow\mathrm{hom}(d,a\otimes(b\otimes c))
. They are represented graphically by

They are also unitary matrices if the fusion category is unitary. In symmetric fusion categories,
R^{\mathrm{T}}
is the inverse of
R
, where
T
denotes transposition with respect to the standard basis.

Let us consider the special case where two objects fuse to the unit object
0\in\mathcal{C}
. This motivates the definition of a dual object. More precisely, for an object
a\in\mathcal{C}
in a monoidal category
\mathcal{C}
, we can define its (left) dual object
a^{*}\in\mathcal{C}
if there exist two morphisms
e_{a}:a^{*}\otimes a\rightarrow 0
(coevaluation) and
i_{a}:0\rightarrow a\otimes a^{*}
(evaluation) satisfying certain consistency relations
[
41
, Sec. 3.3]
. The dual object is represented graphically by an arrow pointing downwards so that we have the relation
a^{*}
=
a

.

The coevaluation and evaluation morphisms are depicted as
e_{a}
a
a^{*}
0
and
i_{a}
a
a^{*}
0

.

Since the unit object
0\in\mathcal{C}
is self-dual, we omit its arrow and draw it as a dashed line
0

.

Right dual objects are defined analogously. A monoidal category is called
rigid
(or sometimes
compact
) if every object
a\in\mathcal{C}
has both a left and a right dual object. In braided monoidal categories, the existence of a left dual already ensures the existence of a right dual
[
60
, Prop. 7.2]
. Notice that
e_{a}
and
i_{a}
are just special cases of fusion and splitting morphisms. The existence of coevaluation and evaluation morphisms allow the definition of a trace, see Ref.
[
43
, Ch. 4.7]
. For a morphism
f\in\mathrm{hom}(a,a^{**})
, the trace is represented graphically by

Now, let
\mathcal{C}
be a rigid fusion category. If every object
a\in\mathcal{C}
admits a natural isomorphism
p_{a}:a\simeq a^{**}
called
pivotal structure
, then
\mathcal{C}
is called
pivotal
. For an object
a\in\mathcal{C}
, the trace over the pivotal structure is called the
quantum dimension
. We denote it by
d_{a}\equiv\mathrm{tr}(p_{a})
and use the simplified graphical notation

where we omit drawing the unit object. Notice that we have
d_{a}=d_{a^{**}}
for all
a\in\mathcal{C}

[
43
, Ch. 4.7]
. If, in addition,
d_{a}=d_{a^{*}}
for all
a\in\mathcal{C}
, then
\mathcal{C}
is called
spherical
, see Ref.
[
61
]
. Examples are unitary fusion categories, which are always spherical
[
62
, Prop. 8.23]
. Furthermore, every braided pivotal category is automatically spherical
[
61
]
. In fact, the pivotal structure behaves particularly nicely in braided categories: Let
\mathcal{C}
be a rigid braided monoidal category. Then
\mathcal{C}
is called
ribbon

[
63
]
(or sometimes
balanced
), if there exists a
twist
. That is, a family of natural isomorphisms
\theta_{a}:a\rightarrow a
for all
a\in\mathcal{C}
graphically denoted as
a

.

It is easy to see that the pivotal structure defines a twist and vice versa. Hence, every rigid braided monoidal category is ribbon if and only if it is pivotal. In every ribbon fusion category, the
F
-symbols satisfy an algebraic symmetry property
[
49
, Appendix F]

\displaystyle\big(F_{d}^{abc}\big)_{ef}

\displaystyle=\big(F_{c^{*}}^{bad^{*}}\big)_{ef^{*}}=\big(F_{a^{*}}^{d^{*}cb}\big)_{e^{*}f}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{d_{e}d_{f}}{d_{a}d_{c}}}\big(F_{d^{*}}^{e^{*}bf^{*}}\big)_{a^{*}c^{*}}

called
tetrahedral symmetry

[
50
]
. This symmetry comes from evaluating a tetrahedral string diagram in different ways and imposing that all of them provide the same value
[
42
, Sec. 8.1]
. It can be shown by tetrahedral symmetry
[
42
, Sec. 8.1]
that the following graphical relation holds:

To conclude, let us summarize the above discussion and refer back to the setting of the volume operator. Here, we assumed that
\mathcal{C}
is a ribbon fusion category (also called
premodular category)
. Notice that we necessarily need to work in a rigid braided monoidal category to ensure that the diagram defining the volume operator is well-defined. The above discussion shows that we only added two mild assumptions to this minimal requirement: First, we demand that
\mathcal{C}
be a fusion category to guarantee that the
F
- and
R
-symbols are well-defined and to ensure that the volume operator has a matrix representation in the standard basis (
53
). Second, we assume that
\mathcal{C}
is pivotal. It is worth pointing out that it has been conjectured that every fusion category is pivotal
[
62
]
, and there are currently no known examples of non-pivotal fusion categories. Therefore, this assumption is not restrictive. Nonetheless, due to the existence of a braiding, the pivotality of
\mathcal{C}
implies sphericality as well as tetrahedral symmetry of the
F
-symbols.
